					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Pope John Paul I</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 107 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-107" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">107 languages</span>

						<div id="siteSub" class="noprint">From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia</div>

					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"><span class="mw-redirectedfrom">(Redirected from <a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Paul_I&amp;redirect=no" class="mw-redirect" title="John Paul I">John Paul I</a>)</span></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Head of the Catholic Church in 1978</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"John Paul I" redirects here. Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="John Paul (disambiguation)">John Paul Sr.</a> or <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="John Paul (disambiguation)">John Paul</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>

<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header"><div style="font-weight:bold; background-color: #FFCF00 ;">

border-collapse:collapse; border-spacing:0px; border:none; width:100%; margin:0px; font-size:100%; clear:none; float:none"></table></td></tr></tbody></table> 
<p><b>Pope John Paul I</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes Paulus I</i>; <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Giovanni Paolo I</i>; born <b>Albino Luciani</b> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="it-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Italian" title="Help:IPA/Italian">[alˈbiːno luˈtʃaːni]</a></span>; 17 October 1912 – 28 September 1978) was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and sovereign of the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican City</a> from 26 August 1978 until his death 33 days later. His reign is among the shortest in papal history, resulting in the most recent <a href="/wiki/Year_of_three_popes" title="Year of three popes">year of three popes</a> and the first to occur since 1605. John Paul I remains the most recent Italian-born <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">pope</a>, the last in a succession of such popes that started with <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Clement VII</a> in 1523.
</p><p>Before the <a href="/wiki/August_1978_papal_conclave" title="August 1978 papal conclave">August 1978 papal conclave</a> that elected him, he expressed his desire not to be elected, telling those close to him that he would decline the papacy if elected; upon the cardinals' electing him, he felt an obligation to say yes.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He was the first pontiff to have a double name, choosing "John Paul" in honour of his two immediate predecessors, <a href="/wiki/John_XXIII" class="mw-redirect" title="John XXIII">John XXIII</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paul_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul VI">Paul VI</a>. He explained that he was indebted to John XXIII and to Paul VI for naming him a <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a>, respectively. Furthermore, he was the first pope to add the regnal number "I", designating himself "the First".
</p><p>His two immediate successors, <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_II" class="mw-redirect" title="John Paul II">John Paul II</a> and <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XVI" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XVI">Benedict XVI</a>, later recalled the warm qualities of the late pontiff in several addresses. In Italy, he is remembered with the appellatives of <span title="Italian-language text"><i lang="it">Il Papa del Sorriso</i></span> (<abbr title="translation">transl.</abbr><span>&#8201;<b>The Smiling Pope</b></span>)<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2004_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2004-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and <span title="Italian-language text"><i lang="it">Il Sorriso di Dio</i></span> (<abbr title="translation">transl.</abbr><span>&#8201;The Smile of God</span>).<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)">Time</a></i> magazine and other publications referred to him as "<b>The September Pope</b>".<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He is also known in Italy as "Papa Luciani". In his hometown of <a href="/wiki/Canale_d%27Agordo" title="Canale d&#39;Agordo">Canale d'Agordo</a> a museum built and named in his honor is dedicated to his life and brief papacy.
</p><p>He was declared a <a href="/wiki/Servant_of_God" title="Servant of God">servant of God</a> by his successor, John Paul II, on 23 November 2003, the first step on the road to sainthood. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> confirmed his <a href="/wiki/Heroic_virtue" title="Heroic virtue">heroic virtue</a> on 8 November 2017 and named him as <a href="/wiki/Venerable" class="mw-redirect" title="Venerable">Venerable</a>. Pope Francis presided over the <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> on 4&#160;September 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg/150px-Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="199" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg/225px-Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg/300px-Young_Albino_Luciani_%E2%80%93_unframed.jpg 2x" data-file-width="971" data-file-height="1287" /></a><figcaption>Albino Luciani approximately at the age of 10, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1922–23</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Albino Luciani was born on 17 October 1912 in <a href="/wiki/Canale_d%27Agordo" title="Canale d&#39;Agordo">Forno di Canale</a> (now Canale d'Agordo) in <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Belluno" title="Province of Belluno">Belluno</a>, a province of the <a href="/wiki/Veneto" title="Veneto">Veneto</a> region in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Italy" title="Northern Italy">Northern Italy</a>. He was the son of Giovanni Luciani (c. 1872–1952), a <a href="/wiki/Bricklayer" title="Bricklayer">bricklayer</a>, and Bortola Tancon (c. 1879–1947). Albino was followed by two brothers, Federico (1915–1916) and Edoardo (1917–2008), and a sister, Antonia (1920–2010). He was <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptised</a> on the day he was born by the <a href="/wiki/Midwife" title="Midwife">midwife</a> because he was considered to be in danger of death. The solemn rites of baptism were formalised in the <a href="/wiki/Parish_church" title="Parish church">parish church</a> two days later.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luciani was a restless child. In 1922, aged 10, he was awestruck when a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">Capuchin</a> friar came to his village to preach the <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lenten</a> sermons. From that moment, he decided that he wanted to become a priest and went to his father to ask for his permission. His father agreed and said to him: "I hope that when you become a priest you will be on the side of the workers, for Christ Himself would have been on their side".<sup id="cite_ref-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luciani entered the <a href="/wiki/Minor_seminary" title="Minor seminary">minor seminary</a> of <a href="/wiki/Feltre" title="Feltre">Feltre</a> in 1923, where his teachers found him "too lively", and later went on to the <a href="/wiki/Seminary" title="Seminary">major seminary</a> of Belluno. During his stay at Belluno, he attempted to join the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuits</a>. However, he was denied by the seminary's <a href="/wiki/Rector_(ecclesiastical)" title="Rector (ecclesiastical)">rector</a>, Bishop <a href="/wiki/Giosu%C3%A8_Cattarossi" title="Giosuè Cattarossi">Giosuè Cattarossi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ordination_and_teaching_career">Ordination and teaching career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Ordination and teaching career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">Ordained</a> a <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Priesthood (Catholic Church)">priest</a> on 7 July 1935, Luciani then served as a <a href="/wiki/Curate" title="Curate">curate</a> in his native Forno de Canale before becoming a professor and the vice-rector of the Belluno seminary in 1937.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Among the different subjects, he taught <a href="/wiki/Dogmatic_theology" title="Dogmatic theology">dogmatic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ethics_in_religion" title="Ethics in religion">moral theology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">canon law</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sacred_art" class="mw-redirect" title="Sacred art">sacred art</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1941, Luciani started to work on a <a href="/wiki/Doctorate_of_Sacred_Theology" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctorate of Sacred Theology">Doctorate of Sacred Theology</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_Gregorian_University" title="Pontifical Gregorian University">Pontifical Gregorian University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> This required at least one year's attendance in Rome. However, the Belluno seminary's superiors wanted him to continue teaching during his doctoral studies. The situation was resolved by a special <a href="/wiki/Dispensation_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dispensation (Catholic Church)">dispensation</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> on 27 March 1941. His <a href="/wiki/Thesis" title="Thesis">thesis</a> (<i>The origin of the human soul according to <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Rosmini-Serbati" class="mw-redirect" title="Antonio Rosmini-Serbati">Antonio Rosmini</a></i>) largely attacked Rosmini's theology and earned him his doctorate <i><a href="/wiki/Latin_honors" title="Latin honors">magna cum laude</a></i> in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1947, he was named chancellor to Bishop <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Bortignon" title="Girolamo Bortignon">Girolamo Bortignon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">OFM Cap</a>, of Belluno,<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and was appointed a Supernumerary Privy Chamberlain of His Holiness, the most junior class of papal prelate, on 15 December.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In 1954, he was named the <a href="/wiki/Vicar_general" title="Vicar general">vicar general</a> for the Belluno diocese.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Luciani was nominated for the position of bishop several times, but he was passed over each time due to his poor health, stature, and resigned appearance. In 1949, he published a book titled <i>Catechesis in crumbs</i>. This book, his first, was about teaching the truths of the faith in a simple way, directly and comprehensible to all people.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Episcopate">Episcopate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Episcopate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 15 December 1958, Luciani was appointed <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Vittorio_Veneto" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Vittorio Veneto">Bishop of Vittorio Veneto</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John XXIII</a>. He received his <a href="/wiki/Bishop_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop (Catholic Church)">episcopal consecration</a> later that month from Pope John XXIII himself, with Bishops Bortignon and <a href="/wiki/Gioacchino_Muccin" title="Gioacchino Muccin">Gioacchino Muccin</a> serving as the co-<a href="/wiki/Consecrator" title="Consecrator">consecrators</a>. Luciani took possession of the diocese on 11 January 1959, with <i>Humilitas</i> (<i>Humility</i>) as his episcopal motto.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In his first address to his new diocese, he told the people that he sought to be "a bishop who is a teacher and a servant".<sup id="cite_ref-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a bishop, he participated in all the sessions of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a> (1962–1965). On 18 April 1962, Luciani issued a pastoral letter, entitled "Notes on the Council", to alert the faithful to the structure of the proceedings and the overall purpose of the Council, chiefly, the doctrinal and practical issues.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1965 and 1969, he faced the <a href="/wiki/Schism_of_Montaner" title="Schism of Montaner">schism of Montaner</a>: almost all the residents of Montaner, a <a href="/wiki/Frazione" title="Frazione">frazione</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sarmede" title="Sarmede">Sarmede</a>, decided to renounce <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholicism</a> and embrace the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a> religion, because they had a great disagreement with their bishop, Luciani. The people disagreed with Luciani's decision to appoint John Gava as a new priest in 1966 since the people wanted their own choice, rather than the one Luciani had settled on. The people then wanted a compromise: make their choice the parish's vice-rector if not parish priest. However, Monsignor Luciani said the small village needed only one priest, and he was the sole authority on priestly selection. Continually, he recommended new priests, but each was denied by the people. Finally, he was escorted by the police and took the Eucharist from the Montaner church, leaving the church unblessed and waiting for their next move.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1966, Luciani visited <a href="/wiki/Burundi" title="Burundi">Burundi</a> in East Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albino_Luciani,_1969_(3).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg/150px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg/225px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg/300px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1969_%283%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="439" data-file-height="513" /></a><figcaption>Albino Luciani in 1969</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 15 December 1969, Luciani was appointed the new <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Venice" title="Patriarch of Venice">patriarch of Venice</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a>, taking possession of his new archdiocese the following February. That same month he received honorary citizenship of the town of Vittorio Veneto, where he had previously served as bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1971_Synod_of_Bishops">1971 Synod of Bishops</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: 1971 Synod of Bishops">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/Second_Ordinary_General_Assembly_of_the_Synod_of_Bishops" title="Second Ordinary General Assembly of the Synod of Bishops">Synod of Bishops</a> held in Rome in 1971, to which he was personally invited by the pope, Luciani suggested to the bishops assembled that dioceses in countries that were heavily industrialised should relinquish around 1% of all their income to Third World nations to be given "not as alms, but something that is owed. Owed to compensate for the injustices that our consumer-oriented world is committing towards the 'world on the way to development' and to in some way make reparation for social sin, of which we must become aware".<sup id="cite_ref-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Life_of_Albino_Luciani-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinalate">Cardinalate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Cardinalate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pope Paul VI created Luciani the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-Priest" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal-Priest">Cardinal-Priest</a> of <a href="/wiki/San_Marco_(Rome)" class="mw-redirect" title="San Marco (Rome)">San Marco</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_consistory" title="Papal consistory">consistory</a> on 5 March 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-vaticanbio_10-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaticanbio-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as Patriarch of Venice, Luciani clashed with priests who supported the liberalisation of divorce in Italy, eventually suspending some of them.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> At the same time, he was opposed to the 1974 referendum restricting divorce after it had been liberalised, feeling that such a move would fail and simply point out a divided Church with declining influence.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albino_Luciani,_1973_(2).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg/220px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg/330px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg/440px-Albino_Luciani%2C_1973_%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2307" data-file-height="1549" /></a><figcaption>Pope Paul VI makes Luciani a cardinal in 1973.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1975, Luciani travelled to <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> in May. Later that year (6–21 November), he visited <a href="/wiki/Brazil" title="Brazil">Brazil</a>, where he met with members of the clergy, including <a href="/wiki/Alo%C3%ADsio_Lorscheider" title="Aloísio Lorscheider">Aloísio Lorscheider</a>. Upon returning to Italy, he suffered an <a href="/wiki/Embolus" title="Embolus">embolus</a> in his right eye. Luciani also visited <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A1tima,_Portugal" title="Fátima, Portugal">Fatima</a> a few months later. While there, he met with Sister <a href="/wiki/Lucia_dos_Santos" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucia dos Santos">Lucia dos Santos</a>, the surviving visionary of three children who in 1917 claimed to see apparitions of the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a> (revered in this form under the title <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Fatima" class="mw-redirect" title="Our Lady of Fatima">Our Lady of Fatima</a>). When Luciani met Sister Lucia, she referred to him as "Holy Father". This greeting shocked the humble cardinal.<sup id="cite_ref-uscatholic.org_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uscatholic.org-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In January 1976, he published <i><a href="/wiki/Illustrissimi" title="Illustrissimi">Illustrissimi</a></i> ("To the Illustrious Ones"), a collection of letters penned by him in previous years, whimsically addressed to historical and literary figures such as <a href="/wiki/Charles_Dickens" title="Charles Dickens">Dickens</a>, <a href="/wiki/G._K._Chesterton" title="G. K. Chesterton">G. K. Chesterton</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maria_Theresa_of_Austria" class="mw-redirect" title="Maria Theresa of Austria">Maria Theresa of Austria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Teresa_of_Avila" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Teresa of Avila">Saint Teresa of Avila</a>, <a href="/wiki/Goethe" class="mw-redirect" title="Goethe">Goethe</a>, Figaro, <a href="/wiki/Pinocchio" title="Pinocchio">Pinocchio</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Pickwick_Club" class="mw-redirect" title="Pickwick Club">Pickwick Club</a>, <a href="/wiki/King_David" class="mw-redirect" title="King David">King David</a>, and Jesus.
</p><p>In 1975, he suggested disciplinary punishment for priests who spoke out in favor of the Communist Party or other leftist groups.<sup id="cite_ref-The_New_Pope:_John_Paul_I_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_New_Pope:_John_Paul_I-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1976, Luciani sold a gold cross and pectoral gold chain that Pope John XXIII had given to him (which once belonged to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> before him) to raise money for disabled children.<sup id="cite_ref-Herald_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Herald-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He also urged fellow priests in Venice to sell their valuables to contribute to this cause and as a way for them to live simply and humbly.<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> As Patriarch of Venice, Luciani established family counseling clinics to assist the poor in coping with marital, financial and sexual problems.
</p><p>In 1978 he forbade the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Mass" title="Tridentine Mass">Tridentine Mass</a> in the Archdiocese of Venice.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election">Election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/August_1978_papal_conclave" title="August 1978 papal conclave">August 1978 papal conclave</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Juan_Pablo_I.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Juan_Pablo_I.jpg/220px-Juan_Pablo_I.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Juan_Pablo_I.jpg/330px-Juan_Pablo_I.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Juan_Pablo_I.jpg/440px-Juan_Pablo_I.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1800" data-file-height="1198" /></a><figcaption>First appearance of Pope John Paul I following his election on 26 August 1978</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pope Paul VI died on 6 August 1978, ending a reign of fifteen years. Luciani was summoned to Rome for the conclave to elect the new pope. Luciani was not considered <a href="/wiki/Papabile" title="Papabile">papabile</a>, though mentioned on occasion in several papers.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> However, a few cardinals approached him with their opinion that he would make a fine pontiff.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Luciani was elected on the fourth ballot of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_August_1978" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, August 1978">August 1978</a> <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave" title="Papal conclave">papal conclave</a>. Luciani had previously said to his secretary, Father <a href="/wiki/Diego_Lorenzi" title="Diego Lorenzi">Diego Lorenzi</a> and Father <a href="/wiki/Prospero_Grech" class="mw-redirect" title="Prospero Grech">Prospero Grech</a> (later a cardinal himself), that he would decline the papacy if elected, and that he intended to vote for <a href="/wiki/Alo%C3%ADsio_Lorscheider" title="Aloísio Lorscheider">Aloísio Lorscheider</a>, whom he met in <a href="/wiki/Brazil" title="Brazil">Brazil</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Jaime_Sin" title="Jaime Sin">Jaime Sin</a> of the Philippines told him: "You will be the new pope."<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, when he was asked by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Marie_Villot" title="Jean-Marie Villot">Jean-Marie Villot</a> if he accepted his election, Luciani replied, "May God forgive you for what you have done", but accepted the election. After his election, when Sin paid him homage, the new pope said: "You were a prophet, but my reign will be a short one".<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> On the balcony of St Peter's Basilica, <a href="/wiki/Pericle_Felici" title="Pericle Felici">Pericle Felici</a> announced that the cardinals had elected Albino Luciani, Patriarch of Venice, who had chosen the name Pope John Paul I.<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> It was the first time that a pope chose a double name. He later explained that the double name was taken to gratefully honour his two immediate predecessors: <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">John XXIII</a>, who had named him a bishop, and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Paul VI</a>, who had named him <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Venice" title="Patriarch of Venice">Patriarch of Venice</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">Cardinal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He was also the first pope to designate himself "the First" with the name.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a>, elected in 2013, also took a previously unused papal name but chose not to be called "the First".)
</p><p>In the aftermath of the election, the pope confided to his brother Edoardo that his first thought was to call himself "Pius XIII" in honour of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a>, but he gave up on the idea, worried that the traditionalist members of the Church might exploit this choice of regnal name.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Observers have suggested that his selection was a compromise to satisfy rumoured divisions among seemingly rival camps within the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Conservatism" title="Conservatism">Conservatives</a> and <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Curialists</a> supporting <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Siri" title="Giuseppe Siri">Giuseppe Siri</a>, who favoured a more conservative interpretation or even reversal of controversial ideas being promoted as "in the spirit of <a href="/wiki/Vatican_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Vatican II">Vatican II</a>" but which had never been discussed at the recent pastoral council.</li>
<li>Those who favoured a more liberal interpretation of Vatican II's reforms along with some Italian cardinals who supported <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Benelli" title="Giovanni Benelli">Giovanni Benelli</a>, who had created some opposition due to alleged "<a href="/wiki/Autocracy" title="Autocracy">autocratic</a>" tendencies.</li>
<li>The cardinals within the increasingly international <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a>, beyond the Italians who were experiencing diminished influence, such as <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Karol Wojtyła</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>During the days following the conclave, the cardinals were generally elated at the reaction to Pope John Paul I, some of them happily saying that they had elected "God's candidate".<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Argentina" title="Argentina">Argentine</a> <a href="/wiki/Eduardo_Francisco_Pironio" title="Eduardo Francisco Pironio">Eduardo Francisco Pironio</a> stated, "We were witnesses of a moral miracle."<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Mother_Teresa" title="Mother Teresa">Mother Teresa</a>, commenting about the new pope, "He has been the greatest gift of God, a sun beam of God's love shining in the darkness of the world."<sup id="cite_ref-papaluciani.com_21-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papaluciani.com-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> British primate <a href="/wiki/Basil_Cardinal_Hume" class="mw-redirect" title="Basil Cardinal Hume">Basil Hume</a> declared: "Once it had happened, it seemed totally and entirely right ... We felt as if our hands were being guided as we wrote his name on the paper".<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A dramatic event, soon after the election, occurred when the leader of the delegation from the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nikodim_(Rotov)" title="Nikodim (Rotov)">Metropolitan Nikodim (Rotov) of Leningrad</a>, collapsed and died after a ceremony on 5 September 1978. The new pope immediately came over and prayed for him.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Church_policies">Church policies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Church policies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Six-point_plan">Six-point plan</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Six-point plan">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>After he became pope, he had set six plans down which would dictate his pontificate:
</p>
<ul><li>To renew the church through the policies implemented by Vatican II.</li>
<li>To revise canon law.</li>
<li>To remind the church of its duty to preach the Gospel.</li>
<li>To promote church unity without watering down doctrine.</li>
<li>To promote dialogue.</li>
<li>To encourage world peace and social justice.<sup id="cite_ref-Google_Books_16-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Google_Books-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Humanising_the_papacy">Humanising the papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Humanising the papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ioannes_Paulus_I,_at_the_window,_1978_(retouched)_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-Ioannes_Paulus_I%2C_at_the_window%2C_1978_%28retouched%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2251" data-file-height="3376" /></a><figcaption>John Paul I photographed from his <a href="/wiki/Papal_apartments" title="Papal apartments">study</a> window in 1978</figcaption></figure>
<p>After his election, John Paul I quickly made several decisions that would "humanise" the office of the pope. He was the first modern pope to speak in the singular form, using 'I' instead of the <a href="/wiki/Pluralis_majestatis" class="mw-redirect" title="Pluralis majestatis">royal <i>we</i></a>. However, the official records of his speeches were often rewritten in a more formal style by aides, who reinstated the royal <i>we</i> in press releases and <i><a href="/wiki/L%27Osservatore_Romano" title="L&#39;Osservatore Romano">L'Osservatore Romano</a></i>. He initially refused to use the <i><a href="/wiki/Sedia_gestatoria" title="Sedia gestatoria">sedia gestatoria</a></i> until others convinced him of its need to allow himself to be seen by crowds. He was the last pope to use it. He was the first pope to refuse to be crowned.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Instead of a coronation, he inaugurated his papacy with a "<a href="/wiki/Papal_inauguration" title="Papal inauguration">papal inauguration</a>" where he received the papal <a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">pallium</a> as the symbol of his position as Bishop of Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Moral_theology">Moral theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Moral theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Moral_theology_of_John_Paul_I" title="Moral theology of John Paul I">Moral theology of John Paul I</a></div>
<p>British researcher Paul Spackman described Luciani as a man of "doctrinal rigor leavened by pastoral and social open-mindednes," who left behind a "legacy of gentle and compassionate bridge-building."<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Contraception">Contraception</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Contraception">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Luciani had mixed feelings regarding the traditional stance on contraception. In 1968, as Bishop of Vittorio Veneto, he submitted a report to his predecessor as the Patriarch of Venice, <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Urbani" title="Giovanni Urbani">Giovanni Urbani</a>, that argued that the contraceptive pill should be permitted. It was agreed on by fellow Veneto bishops and was later submitted to Pope Paul VI.<sup id="cite_ref-John_Julius_Norwich_2011,_p._445_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Julius_Norwich_2011,_p._445-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> When <i><a href="/wiki/Humanae_vitae" title="Humanae vitae">Humanae vitae</a></i> was released, re-affirming the teaching of the Church against artificial contraception, Luciani defended that document. Nevertheless, he seemed to contradict that defence in a letter he wrote to his diocese four days after the encyclical's release.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In May 1978, Luciani was invited to speak at a Milanese conference to celebrate the 10th anniversary of the encyclical. He refused to speak at the event or even attend it.<sup id="cite_ref-John_Julius_Norwich_2011,_p._445_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Julius_Norwich_2011,_p._445-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Raymond and Lauretta take a different view, saying that while serving as Patriarch of Venice, "Luciani was intransigent with his upholding of the teaching of the Church and severe with those, who through intellectual pride and disobedience paid no attention to the Church's prohibition of contraception, though while not condoning the sin, he was patient with those who sincerely tried and failed to live up to the Church's teaching."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2004_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2004-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Abortion">Abortion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Abortion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In his letter to Carlo Goldoni from the book <i><a href="/wiki/Illustrissimi" title="Illustrissimi">Illustrissimi</a></i>, Luciani took a critical perspective of abortion and argued that it violated God's law and that it went against the deepest aspirations of women, profoundly disturbing them.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Artificial_insemination">Artificial insemination</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Artificial insemination">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In an interview before the death of Pope Paul VI in 1978, when asked for his reaction to the birth of the first test-tube baby <a href="/wiki/Louise_Brown" title="Louise Brown">Louise Brown</a>, Luciani expressed concerns about the possibility that artificial insemination could lead to women being used as "baby factories", but he refused to condemn the parents,<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> noting that they simply wanted to have a baby.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His view was that "from every side the press is sending its congratulations to the English couple and best wishes to their baby girl. In imitation of God, who desires and loves human life, I too offer my best wishes to the baby girl. As for her parents, I do not have any right to condemn them; subjectively, if they have acted with the right intention and in good faith, they may even obtain great merit before God for what they have decided on and asked the doctors to carry out." Luciani added, "Getting down, however, to the act in itself, and good faith aside, the moral problem which is posed is: is extrauterine fertilization in vitro or in a test tube, licit?... I do not find any valid reasons to deviate from this norm, by declaring licit the separation of the transmission of life from the marriage act."<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Divorce">Divorce</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Divorce">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1969, Luciani was cautious of de facto relationships as a lesser evil to divorce. He said that unions like those should not be the same as marriage. However, he added that "there are, in undeniably pathological family situations, painful cases. To remedy that, some propose a divorce, which, conversely, would aggravate this. But some remedy outside of divorce, you can't really find? Once the legitimate family is protected and made a place of honor, you will not be able to recognise with all appropriate precautions some civil effect to de facto unions."<sup id="cite_ref-Vatican_Insider_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican_Insider-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Homosexuality">Homosexuality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Homosexuality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In a 1974 interview, while he was the Patriarch of Venice, Luciani upheld the traditional line: "A sexuality that is worthy of man must be a part of love for a person of a different sex with the added commitments of fidelity and indissolubility."<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ordination_of_women">Ordination of women</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Ordination of women">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In a 1975 talk Luciani gave to a group of sisters, he expressed his views on the ordination of women into the priesthood:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>You will ask: what about ... the priesthood itself? I can say to you: Christ bestowed the pastoral ministry on men alone, on his apostles. Did he mean this to be valid only for a short time, almost as though he made allowances for the prejudice about the inferiority of women prevalent in his time? Or did he intend it to be valid always? Let it be very clear: Christ never accepted the prejudice about the inferiority of women: they are always admirable figures in the Gospels, more so than the apostles themselves. The priesthood, however, is a service given by means of spiritual powers and not a form of superiority. Through the will of Christ, women—in my judgment—carry out a different, complementary, and precious service in the church, but they are not "possible priests" ... That does not do wrong to women.<sup id="cite_ref-ncr2012_4-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncr2012-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Communism">Communism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Communism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John Paul I reiterated the official views of the church regarding <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxism</a> and Catholicism being incompatible and believed it to be a "weapon to disobey" the Christian faith. As Patriarch of Venice, he struggled at times with Marxist students who were demanding changes in Venetian policies. He also forbade those factions that were Marxist from threatening the faith.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Interfaith_dialogue">Interfaith dialogue</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Interfaith dialogue">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Islam">Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>John Paul I was a friend to the Muslim people and, as <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Venice" title="Patriarch of Venice">Patriarch of Venice</a>, said to Catholics that faithful Muslims had the "right to build a <a href="/wiki/Mosque" title="Mosque">mosque</a>" to practice their faith in the archdiocese. In November 1964, he explained the declaration of <a href="/wiki/Dignitatis_humanae" title="Dignitatis humanae">Dignitatis humanae</a>: "There are 4,000 Muslims in Rome: they have the right to build a mosque. There is nothing to say: you have to let them do it".<sup id="cite_ref-Vatican_Insider_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vatican_Insider-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Universal_call_to_holiness">Universal call to holiness</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Universal call to holiness">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Luciani stressed the need throughout his time as Bishop of Vittorio Veneto to answer the <a href="/wiki/Universal_call_to_holiness" title="Universal call to holiness">universal call to holiness</a> as was an invitation in the Second Vatican Council. He believed that sainthood was something that all Catholics could achieve if they led a life of service to God. Luciani said there were no barriers to sainthood and discussed this theme of the council in a homily on 6 January 1962: "We are called by God to be true saints". Luciani stressed the importance of this and said God invites Catholics and obligates them to sainthood. He also said that by professing love for God, Catholics say: "my God I want to be holy, I will strive to be holy".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mercy">Mercy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Mercy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During his brief pontificate, John Paul I spoke three times on the concept of God's mercy. In his General Audience address on 13 September 1978, the pope said that the entire point of mercy is "to surrender to God" through faith in Him, which goes about "transforming one's life" in the fight against sin, and the pursuit of holiness. The pope continued that "God has so much tenderness for us" in which "He begs me to repent" from sin to return to God's embrace. The pope concluded that "the Church too must be good; good to everyone" in its outreach to the faithful.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Paul I, in his Angelus address on 24 September 1978, spoke about the importance of doing good deeds through charitable and merciful acts in society, to make the world more just, and to improve the overall conditions of society. The pope elaborated that it was important to "try to be good and to infect others with a goodness imbued with the meekness and love taught by Christ" while seeking to give our all in service to others. The pope further points out Christ's example on the Cross, in which he forgave and excused those who persecuted, referring to it as a sentiment which "would help society so much" if put into constant practice.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The pope also spoke about mercy in his address at the General Audience on 27 September 1978. He referred to God as "infinite good" capable of providing for our "eternal happiness" in His love for us. John Paul I continued that it may be "difficult to love others; we do not find them likeable, they have offended us and hurt us", though says that forgiveness between brothers and sisters is very important for unity and peace among people. Additionally, the pope referred to the seven corporal and spiritual acts of mercy, which he said acted as a guide for Christians, though highlighting the fact that "the list is not complete and it would be necessary to update it" as times change since global situations change. The pope concluded that justice adds to charity, which is linked to the theme of mercy.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Interpretation_of_Vatican_II">Interpretation of Vatican II</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Interpretation of Vatican II">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px .ambox-delete{border-left:10px solid #b32424}.mw-parser-output .ambox-content{border-left:10px solid #f28500}.mw-parser-output .ambox-style{border-left:10px solid #fc3}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Pope_John_Paul_I" title="Special:EditPage/Pope John Paul I">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">June 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Luciani had attended all sessions of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a> (1962–65) while he was the Bishop of Vittorio Veneto. He had hoped that the council would highlight "Christian optimism" in terms of Christ's teachings against the culture of relativism. He denounced a fundamental ignorance of the "basic elements of the faith"—it was this point that he wished to focus on as opposed to secularism throughout the world.
</p><p>In terms of global interpretation of the council, Luciani wrote: "The physiognomy and structure of the Catholic Church have been determined once and for all by the Lord and cannot be touched. If anything, superstructures can. Things that have not been determined by Christ, but were introduced by popes or councils or the faithful, can be changed, or eliminated today or tomorrow. Yesterday they might have introduced a certain number of dioceses, a certain way to lead missions, to educate priests, they might have chosen to follow certain cultural trends. Well, this can be changed and one can say "the Church that comes out of the Council is still the same as it was yesterday, but renewed". No one can ever say "We have a new Church, different from what it was".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In regard to religious freedom, Luciani wrote about the council's declaration, <i>"<a href="/wiki/Dignitatis_humanae" title="Dignitatis humanae">Dignitatis humanae</a>"</i>. In his writings, he said that there is only one true religion that must be followed and no other, affirming that Jesus Christ is the Truth, and that the truth will set one truly free. Though, he said that those that will not accept the one true Catholic Faith, for whatever reason, are indeed free to profess their own religion for various reasons. He makes a clear understanding of true and false liberty. He says that true freedom comes from God, that God makes man free. However, he does continue in repeating the teaching that error does not come from God, and although we are capable of error and sin, and that one who rejects truth cannot be forced to believe it, it is not a God given <i>right</i> to do error. He continues to say that religious freedom must be freely exercised by the individual. He writes that the choice of religion must be a free choice. So he makes clear that for the purposes of keeping peace and order in a diverse society and accepting the free will of man, the freedom of an individual to profess his religion, within certain bounds, is indeed necessary.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="International_travels">International travels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: International travels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 12 September 1978, <a href="/wiki/Mario_Casariego_y_Acevedo" title="Mario Casariego y Acevedo">Mario Casariego y Acevedo</a> of <a href="/wiki/Guatemala" title="Guatemala">Guatemala</a> invited the pope to visit Guatemala in 1979. The pope was said to have thanked him for the invitation but did not provide a response. The week before this, the pope said he was unable to accept an invitation to the <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_Episcopal_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin American Episcopal Conference">Latin American Episcopal Conference</a> in <a href="/wiki/Puebla" title="Puebla">Puebla</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a> for October due to his schedule.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sainthood_causes">Sainthood causes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Sainthood causes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>No saints were <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a> nor people <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> in his brief term on the papal throne, but José Gras y Granollers, <a href="/wiki/John_Vincent_(Carmelite)" title="John Vincent (Carmelite)">Juan Vicente Zengotita-Bengoa Lasuen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Beschin" title="Giuseppe Beschin">Giuseppe Beschin</a> were made <a href="/wiki/Servant_of_God" title="Servant of God">Servants of God</a> during his pontificate on 22 September 1978.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality">Personality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Personality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John Paul I was regarded as a skilled communicator and writer. His book <i><a href="/wiki/Illustrissimi" title="Illustrissimi">Illustrissimi</a></i>, written while he was a cardinal, is a series of letters to a wide collection of historical and fictional persons. Among those still available are his letters to Jesus, King <a href="/wiki/David" title="David">David</a>, Figaro the Barber, Empress <a href="/wiki/Maria_Theresa" title="Maria Theresa">Maria Theresa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pinocchio" title="Pinocchio">Pinocchio</a>. Others "written to" included <a href="/wiki/Mark_Twain" title="Mark Twain">Mark Twain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Dickens" title="Charles Dickens">Charles Dickens</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Marlowe" title="Christopher Marlowe">Christopher Marlowe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> He was also well-read, and was known for reading several newspapers each morning, including one from the Veneto region, before beginning his day.<sup id="cite_ref-FD_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FD-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Paul I impressed people with his personal warmth. He was seen by some as an intellectual lightweight not up to the responsibilities of the papacy, although <a href="/wiki/David_Yallop" title="David Yallop">David Yallop</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/In_God%27s_Name" title="In God&#39;s Name">In God's Name</a></i>) says that this is the result of a <a href="/wiki/Whispering_campaign" title="Whispering campaign">whispering campaign</a> by people in the Vatican who were opposed to Luciani's policies. In the words of <a href="/wiki/John_Cornwell_(writer)" title="John Cornwell (writer)">John Cornwell</a>, "they treated him with condescension"; one senior cleric discussing Luciani said "they have elected <a href="/wiki/Peter_Sellers" title="Peter Sellers">Peter Sellers</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Critics contrasted his sermons mentioning <a href="/wiki/Pinocchio" title="Pinocchio">Pinocchio</a> to the learned intellectual discourses of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pius XII</a> or <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Paul VI</a>. Visitors spoke of his isolation and loneliness and the fact that he was the first pope in decades not to have previously held either a diplomatic role (like <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pius XI</a> and John XXIII) or <a href="/wiki/Roman_Curia" title="Roman Curia">Curial</a> role (like Pius XII and Paul VI) in the Church.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>His personal impact, however, was twofold: his image as a warm, gentle and kind man captivated the whole world. This image was immediately formed when he was presented to the crowd in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Square" title="St. Peter&#39;s Square">St. Peter's Square</a> following his election. The warmth of his presence made him a much-loved figure before he even spoke a word. The media in particular fell under his spell. He was a very skilled <a href="/wiki/Orator" title="Orator">orator</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>According to his aides, he was not the naive idealist his critics made him out to be. <a href="/wiki/Giuseppe_Caprio" title="Giuseppe Caprio">Giuseppe Caprio</a>, the substitute Papal Secretary of State, said that John Paul I quickly accepted his new role and performed it with confidence.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Paul I had admitted that the prospect of the papacy had daunted him to the point that other cardinals had to encourage him to accept it. He refused to have the millennium-old traditional <a href="/wiki/Papal_coronation" title="Papal coronation">papal coronation</a> or wear the <a href="/wiki/Papal_tiara" title="Papal tiara">papal tiara</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> He instead chose to have a simplified <a href="/wiki/Papal_inauguration" title="Papal inauguration">inauguration mass</a>. John Paul I adopted as his <a href="/wiki/Motto" title="Motto">motto</a> the Latin word <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Humilitas</i></span> ('Humility'). In his notable <a href="/wiki/Angelus" title="Angelus">Angelus</a> of 27 August 1978 (delivered on the first full day of his papacy), he impressed the world with his natural friendliness.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sister Margherita Marin, who worked in the Vatican during Luciani's papacy, said in comments made in late 2017 that the pope had admitted the sisters into his apartment chapel for morning Mass, unlike his predecessor Paul VI who had only admitted his secretaries.<sup id="cite_ref-FD_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FD-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Marin also said that Luciani would speak the <a href="/wiki/Venetian_dialect" class="mw-redirect" title="Venetian dialect">Venetian dialect</a> with those Venetian sisters to make them more comfortable, and to better interact with them. The religious also noted that the pope's humor was evident to all those who spoke with him, and he would often joke with the sisters when seeing his picture in the papers: "But you see how they got me", in reference to the quality of his picture.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg/235px-TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg" decoding="async" width="235" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg/353px-TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg/470px-TombaBeatoGiovanniPaoloI2022.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2886" data-file-height="1729" /></a><figcaption>Tomb of John Paul I in the Vatican Grottoes following his 2022 beatification</figcaption></figure>
<p>Around 10 p.m. on the night of his death, the pope learned that several young <a href="/wiki/Neo-Fascists" class="mw-redirect" title="Neo-Fascists">neo-Fascists</a> had fired upon a group of young people reading <i><a href="/wiki/L%27Unit%C3%A0" title="L&#39;Unità">L'Unità</a></i>, the Communist newspaper, outside one of the party's offices in Rome. One boy was killed while another was seriously wounded. The pope lamented to John Magee, "Even the young are killing each other." He later retired to his room to read <a href="/wiki/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis" title="Thomas à Kempis">Thomas à Kempis</a>' <i><a href="/wiki/The_Imitation_of_Christ" title="The Imitation of Christ">The Imitation of Christ</a></i> in bed.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 29 September 1978, on what should have been the 35th day of his pontificate, John Paul I was found dead in his bed with reading material and a bedside lamp still lit. He had probably suffered a <a href="/wiki/Heart_attack" class="mw-redirect" title="Heart attack">heart attack</a> the night before.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Upon the global announcement of the news, <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Zaire" title="Zaire">Zaire</a><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> all declared three days of mourning.
</p><p>John Paul I's funeral was held in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter%27s_Square" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Peter&#39;s Square">Saint Peter's Square</a> on 4 October 1978, celebrated by <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Confalonieri" title="Carlo Confalonieri">Carlo Confalonieri</a>. In his <a href="/wiki/Eulogy" title="Eulogy">eulogy</a> of the late pope, he described him as a flashing comet who briefly lit up the church. He then was laid to rest in the Vatican grottoes.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png/250px-1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="130" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png/375px-1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png/500px-1000_lire_John_Paul_I_-_1978.png 2x" data-file-width="2510" data-file-height="1308" /></a><figcaption>1000 <a href="/wiki/Lira" title="Lira">lire</a> silver coin with a portrait of John Paul I on the front (1978)</figcaption></figure>
<p>There are several <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_I_conspiracy_theories" title="Pope John Paul I conspiracy theories">conspiracy speculations related to his death</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2018_revelations">2018 revelations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: 2018 revelations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The journalist and vice-postulator for John Paul I's cause of canonization, Stefania Falasca, published a new book in 2017 titled <i>Pope Luciani, Chronicle of a Death</i>, in which she revealed that John Paul I had complained of chest pains hours before his death, and the evening before, but paid no attention to it and ordered that his doctor not be called.<sup id="cite_ref-BDC_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BDC-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Falasca confirmed, after interviewing the sisters who found him and documents from the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Apostolic_Archive" title="Vatican Apostolic Archive">Vatican Apostolic Archive</a>, that John Paul I died of a heart attack in the late evening hours of 28 September 1978.<sup id="cite_ref-JPIDC_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JPIDC-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Secretary_of_State" title="Cardinal Secretary of State">Cardinal Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Parolin" title="Pietro Parolin">Pietro Parolin</a>, in his preface for the book, describes the various conspiracies regarding John Paul I's death as little more than "noir reconstructions". Parolin further says that the sudden death of the pope inspired "myriad theories, suspicions, [and] suppositions" based on opinion rather than fact.<sup id="cite_ref-BDC_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BDC-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Falasca noted the 2009 testimony of Sister Margherita Marin, one of the two <a href="/wiki/Nun#Distinction_between_a_nun_and_a_religious_sister" title="Nun">sisters</a> who found the pope dead in his bedroom on the morning of 29 September 1978. John Paul I had made it a practice to have a morning coffee in the <a href="/wiki/Sacristy" title="Sacristy">sacristy</a> and then go into the chapel to pray before tending to the day's matters.<sup id="cite_ref-MJPI_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MJPI-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sister_Vincenza_Taffarel" title="Sister Vincenza Taffarel">Sister Vincenza</a> had noted the pope had not touched the coffee she had left for him in the sacristy at 5:15 am (after about ten minutes) and went looking for him but found him dead, and hastily summoned Marin who also went into the room.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sister Vincenza said: "He hasn't come out yet? Why not?" and knocked a few more times but heard silence, then opened the door and walked in. Marin remained in the hallway but heard the elder sister say: "Your Holiness, you shouldn't pull these jokes on me" because Sister Vincenza also had heart problems.<sup id="cite_ref-BDC_57-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BDC-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MJPI_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MJPI-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-JPIDC_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JPIDC-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Marin testified that John Paul I's hands were cold, and she was struck by the darkness of his nails.<sup id="cite_ref-BDC_57-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BDC-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Marin further testified that original information provided by the Vatican regarding who discovered the pope was wrong, since it had originally been claimed the discovery was by the pope's secretaries Lorenzi and Magee.<sup id="cite_ref-JPIDC_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JPIDC-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Marin testified that "he was in bed with a slight smile" on his face. The reading light over the headboard was still on, with his two pillows under his back propping him up, with his legs outstretched and his arms on top of the bedsheets. John Paul I was still in his <a href="/wiki/Pajamas" title="Pajamas">pajamas</a> with a few typewritten sheets in his hands. His head was slightly turned to the right and his eyes were partially closed; his glasses rested on his nose.<sup id="cite_ref-MJPI_59-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MJPI-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Paul I had suffered a severe pain in his chest for about five minutes around 7:30 pm while reciting the vespers in the chapel with Magee before dinner, but insisted against calling for Doctor Renato Buzzonetti. The latter, the book claimed, was informed of that episode after the pope's death.<sup id="cite_ref-JPIDC_58-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JPIDC-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The book also revealed that, before the conclave that elected John Paul II, the cardinals had sent a series of written questions to the doctors who had embalmed John Paul I either on 10 or 11 October to check if there had been any signs of traumatic injuries, so as to ascertain if he died naturally rather than suspiciously.<sup id="cite_ref-MJPI_59-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MJPI-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Doctor Buzzonetti sent a detailed report to the Cardinal Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Agostino_Casaroli" title="Agostino Casaroli">Agostino Casaroli</a> on 9 October 1979 detailing that the episode of pain John Paul I suffered was in the upper part of the sternal region.<sup id="cite_ref-MJPI_59-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MJPI-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sister Margherita noted in late 2017 in comments made in Belluno that the pope had made a half-hour phone call on the evening of his death to <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Colombo" title="Giovanni Colombo">Giovanni Colombo</a> and said he wanted the <a href="/wiki/Rector_Major_of_the_Salesians" title="Rector Major of the Salesians">Salesian rector major</a> <a href="/wiki/Egidio_Vigan%C3%B2" title="Egidio Viganò">Egidio Viganò</a> to agree to serve as John Paul I's successor as <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Venice" title="Patriarch of Venice">Patriarch of Venice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FD_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FD-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization_process">Canonization process</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_John_Paul_I&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Canonization process">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div
